```json
        "content": "Self-Consistency (SC) majority voting over chain-of-thought traces saturates as sample count grows because same-model traces are correlated voters.\nWe investigate whether symbolic constraint extraction can break this plateau through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts symbolic constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT.\nAcross the full LLM capability range---from Mistral-7B (54\\% SC) to Qwen3-14B thinking mode (86\\% SC)---and three reasoning domains with over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\\sim}$1,250 unique instances), we find that self-extracted constraints do not improve majority voting at any capacity level: none of sixteen non-degenerate conditions shows a significant positive effect; TOST equivalence tests (margin $\\pm 2$\\,pp) establish equivalence for four conditions, while five show genuine SICA--SC differences---four of which are negative.\nWe identify \\emph{symbolic confirmation bias} as the root cause: on incorrect problems, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC.\nProcess-level analysis reveals a deeper bottleneck: chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level Fleiss' $\\kappa$ ($0.41$), indicating that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on answers.\nTwenty-plus same-model remediation strategies spanning fifteen families all fail, revealing a persistent independence-accuracy trade-off at the 7B--32B scale: methods that increase trace independence destroy accuracy (Debate: $\\kappa = 0.15$, $-18.6$\\,pp), while methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor ($\\kappa \\geq 0.40$ across three model families).\nOracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound ($+29.90$\\,pp and $+14.22$\\,pp on FOLIO, $+11.50$\\,pp ProofWriter; $p < 0.001$); same-model self-extraction captures only ${\\sim}$12\\% of the oracle ceiling.\nHowever, a cross-architecture proof-of-concept---DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC---yields $+3.50$\\,pp on ProofWriter ($p = 0.013$; $+10.67$\\,pp at $w{=}5$, $p < 0.001$), confirmed across three NLI architectures, suggesting that the oracle gap is partially recoverable when verification signal comes from outside the reasoning model's distribution.\n",
        "numLines": 9,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 9
```
```json
      }
    },
```

```text
65	
66	\paragraph{Intermediate-SC region.} Mistral-7B on FOLIO (SC $\sim$50--55\%) shows positive directional trends in 2 of 4 seeds ($\Delta = +3.43$\,pp, $p = 0.092$; $+2.94$\,pp, $p = 0.070$); no seed reaches $p < 0.05$ (4-seed mean $+1.47$\,pp). Seeds with identical SC baselines (55.88\%) diverge ($+3.43$ vs.\ $0.00$\,pp), suggesting seed-level constraint quality matters (Appendix~\ref{app:entropy}). Mistral-7B on LogiQA ($\Delta = +0.50$\,pp, $p = 1.0$) shows the same SC range is insufficient when the domain resists formalization (Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}).
67	
68	\paragraph{Type A (SC $\geq$ 60\%): confirmation bias.} All nine Type~A conditions (SC $\geq 60\%$, combined $N > 2{,}800$) produce no significant positive effects. The largest positive trend is LLaMA-3.1-8B on StrategyQA ($\Delta = +2.00$\,pp, $p = 0.424$, n.s.), while Qwen2.5-14B on ProofWriter shows significant degradation ($\Delta = -2.50$\,pp, $p = 0.011$). Even in-context learning with gold FOL examples ($\Delta = +0.50$\,pp, n.s.) cannot break confirmation bias.
69	
70	\paragraph{Type C (SC $\leq$ 40\%): error amplification.} Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($n = 600$, SC $= 39.33\%$) shows $\Delta = -2.83$\,pp ($p = 0.014$): SICA \emph{significantly hurts} by formalizing incorrect reasoning.
71	
72	\paragraph{Degenerate cases.} When $C_0 < 5\%$, nearly all traces agree and SICA reduces to SC by construction.
73	
74	\paragraph{Equivalence testing.} TOST equivalence tests \citep{lakens2017equivalence} with margin $\pm 2$\,pp establish equivalence for 4 of 15 non-degenerate conditions; 5 show genuine SICA--SC differences (4 negative, 1 positive n.s.); the remaining 6 are underpowered (Appendix~\ref{app:tost}).
75	
76	
77	\subsection{Oracle Control}
78	\label{sec:oracle}
79	
80	Oracle variants replace self-extracted constraints with gold annotations: FOL from FOLIO ($n = 204$) and DSL from ProofWriter ($n = 600$). Oracle-SICA yields $+29.90$\,pp on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($p < 10^{-13}$), $+14.22$\,pp on Qwen2.5-14B FOLIO ($p = 2.0 \times 10^{-7}$), and $+11.50$\,pp on ProofWriter ($p < 10^{-14}$). Z3 agrees with gold labels on 95.1\% (FOLIO) and 97.1\% (ProofWriter) of problems. The MAX-SAT mechanism is sound; Mistral-7B's best self-extracted result ($+3.43$\,pp) captures only 11.5\% of its oracle ceiling, with ${\sim}$88\% lost to extraction quality.
```

```text
1	\paragraph{Scale and language.}
2	All models range from 7B to 32B parameters; whether the independence bottleneck persists at larger scales (70B+) or with reasoning-specialized models is untested. All datasets are English-language logical reasoning benchmarks with discrete answer spaces. We do not evaluate trained FOL extractors, external solvers with verified grounding, or multilingual settings. Oracle analysis is limited to FOLIO and ProofWriter (gold annotations available); extending to commonsense reasoning requires constructing comparable gold constraint sets. The cross-architecture NLI verifier (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}) provides no benefit on LogiQA (combo $\Delta \leq +1$\,pp, all $p > 0.5$), indicating that cross-architecture escape requires task-verifier alignment---NLI is effective only when the task naturally decomposes into premise-conclusion entailment. Even on compatible tasks, NLI provides no benefit when SC accuracy exceeds verifier standalone accuracy (Qwen3-14B ProofWriter: SC 85\% vs.\ DeBERTa 36\%, $\Delta \leq +0.50$\,pp, n.s.), confirming that cross-architecture escape requires verifier competitiveness, not merely architectural independence.
3	
4	\paragraph{Statistical power.}
5	The per-label complementarity analysis of the cross-architecture NLI result (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}) involves small cell sizes (e.g., 42 \textsc{False} problems, 69 \textsc{Unknown} problems); per-label McNemar tests would be underpowered. The bias ratio metric ($4.97\times$) was initially computed on a single condition (Mistral-7B FOLIO, $n = 204$); cross-model replication (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}) confirms the pattern but is limited to FOLIO. Four-seed replication shows 2 of 4 seeds yielding null or negative results, and the intermediate-SC region is narrow (SC $\approx$ 50--55\%). The process-level similarity metric uses TF-IDF representations (a lower bound on true process similarity). Pairwise Pearson $r$ between traces (mean $0.625$, Mistral-7B) is monotonically related to Fleiss' $\kappa$ across all four model families, supporting $\kappa$ as a valid consensus proxy despite the simplifying assumptions of Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}.
6	
7	\paragraph{Computational cost trade-off.}
8	SICA requires $4.5\times$ the wall-clock time of SC (81.4 vs.\ 17.7\,min for 204 questions at $K{=}12$), with constraint extraction accounting for 76\% of overhead (\S\ref{sec:exp-setup}). This $3.5\times$ additional cost yields no significant accuracy gain on any condition, making the method impractical in its current form. Alternative prompting strategies (few-shot, chain-of-thought extraction) might improve extraction quality, though the oracle gap suggests the bottleneck is structural. Additional limitations regarding power analysis and deduplication strategy are discussed in Appendix~\ref{app:limitations-extended}.
9	
10	\paragraph{Reproducibility.} All code, data, and experimental scripts will be released upon publication to facilitate replication of SICA, the oracle controls, and all remediation experiments.
11	
```

```text
2	\label{sec:diagnosis}
3

5

46	
47	\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}
48	\label{sec:confirmation-bias}
49	\label{sec:effective-k}
50	
51	Table~\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\kappa$ and Effective-K (Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}) across 15 conditions at $K = 12$. Among instruction-tuned models, $\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, yielding $K_{\text{eff}} = 1.20\text{--}3.62$. Two conditions are jointly necessary for any positive trend: sufficient $K_{\text{eff}}$ ($\leq 1.30$ never improves) and sufficient SC accuracy ($K_{\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\%$ produces significant degradation). Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families produce $\kappa \geq 0.41$ (Table~\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}).
52	
53	This correlation manifests through a specific mechanism: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\times$ compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal.
54	
55	The causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts FOL consistent with it. Constraints from majority-answer traces use trace-specific variable names (56.4\% of cross-answer pairs share zero Z3 variables), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all, and aggregation reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}). Confirmation bias generalizes across model families: bias ratios of $4.97\times$ (Mistral-7B), $3.93\times$ (Qwen2.5-14B), and $2.88{\times}^{\dag}$ (LLaMA-3.1-8B) all significantly exceed $1.0$ ($^{\dag}$based on 4/12 traces due to extraction failures; Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). The oracle ceiling (\S\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that 75.9\% of the potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality.
56	
57	\begin{table}[t]
58	\centering
59	\small
60	\caption{Trace independence metrics ($K = 12$, sorted by $\kappa$ descending). $\kappa$: Fleiss' Kappa. $K_{\text{eff}}$: Effective-K (Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}). SC: Self-Consistency baseline. $\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). {*}\,$p < 0.05$; positive $\Delta$ values do not reach significance. multi-T $= T \in \{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\}$, 4 traces each.}
61	\label{tab:independence}

85	
86	
87	\subsection{Process-Level Independence}
88	\label{sec:process-kappa}
89	
90	Answer-level $\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:
91	\begin{equation}
92	\label{eq:process-sim}
93	\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \sum_{i < j} \cos(\mathbf{v}_i^q, \mathbf{v}_j^q),
94	\end{equation}
95	where $\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\kappa = 0.41$. These measures are uncorrelated ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$): problems with diverse answers do not exhibit diverse reasoning (Figure~\ref{fig:process-kappa}). This gap implies that interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) may spread answers without altering reasoning paths.
96	
97	\begin{figure}[t]
98	\centering
99	\includegraphics[width=0.85\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}
100	\vspace{-2mm}
101	\caption{Answer agreement vs.\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$): process and answer independence are uncorrelated.}
102	\label{fig:process-kappa}
103	\end{figure}
104	
105	
106	\subsection{Regime-Dependent Effects}
107	\label{sec:main-results}
108	\label{sec:diagnostics}
109	
110	SICA effectiveness depends sharply on the accuracy regime (Figure~\ref{fig:regime-scatter}).
111	
112	\paragraph{Intermediate-SC region.} Mistral-7B on FOLIO (SC $\sim$50--55\%) shows positive directional trends in 2 of 4 seeds: $\Delta = +3.43$\,pp ($p = 0.092$) and $+2.94$\,pp ($p = 0.070$); the remaining seeds show null or negative results. No seed reaches $p < 0.05$; the 4-seed mean is $+1.47$\,pp. The default-seed and seed-456 runs share identical SC baselines (55.88\%) yet diverge ($+3.43$ vs.\ $0.00$\,pp), suggesting that seed-level constraint quality, not SC level alone, influences the effect.
113	
114	\paragraph{Type A (SC $\geq$ 60\%).} All nine Type~A conditions produce no significant positive effects. Even in-context learning with gold FOL examples yields $\Delta = +0.50$\,pp (n.s.). Qwen2.5-14B on ProofWriter shows significant degradation ($\Delta = -2.50$\,pp, $p = 0.011$).
115	
116	\paragraph{Type C (SC $\leq$ 40\%).} Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($n = 600$, SC $= 39.33\%$) shows $\Delta = -2.83$\,pp ($p = 0.014$): SICA \emph{significantly hurts} by formalizing incorrect reasoning. When $C_0 < 5\%$, nearly all traces agree and SICA reduces to SC by construction.
117	
118	Four supporting analyses confirm the mechanism: constraint ablation shows MAX-SAT and count-only agree on 94.4\% of predictions ($n = 804$); 91.8\% of constraints are discriminative but mirror the majority; effects concentrate in high-entropy problems ($+12.8$\,pp) with direction determined by accuracy regime; and hyperparameter peaks coincide with low SC baselines (Appendices~\ref{app:extraction}--\ref{app:entropy}).
119	
120	\begin{figure}[t]
121	\centering
122	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{f2_regime_scatter}
123	\caption{SICA $\Delta$ (pp) vs.\ SC baseline. Filled: $n \geq 100$; open: $n < 100$. The intermediate-SC region (50--55\%, shaded) contains the only positive trends (none reaching $p < 0.05$). Type~C: significant degradation ($p = 0.014$).}
124	\label{fig:regime-scatter}
125	\end{figure}
126	
127	
128	\subsection{Remediation: 25+ Strategies Fail}
129	\label{sec:remediation}
130	\label{sec:interventions}
131	
132	We test more than twenty remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.
133	
134	\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail. Contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints from 8.2\% to 6.0\%, but newly discriminative constraints still mirror the majority ($\Delta = -1.1$\,pp). Cross-model extraction fails in both directions: weaker models yield 0\% discriminative constraints; stronger models produce accuracy within $\pm 1$\,pp of self-extraction. Multi-temperature sampling ($T \in \{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\}$) yields $\Delta = 0.00$\,pp.
135	
136	\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} ICG ($\Delta \approx 0$, $p = .206$), Contrastive ($\Delta = -9.31$\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($\Delta = -18.63$/$-23.00$\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail (Table~\ref{tab:method_interventions}, Appendix~\ref{app:remediation}). Ten further experiments (code verification, iterative refinement, logprob filtering, cross-model ensembles) produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:additional_interventions}).
137	
138	\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} compares Fleiss' $\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families (Figure~\ref{fig:kappa-accuracy}). SC ($\kappa = 0.44$), Diverse-Prompt ($\kappa = 0.40$), and ICG ($\kappa = 0.47$) cluster at $\kappa \approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\,pp. All three families cluster above $\kappa = 0.40$, forming a same-model trade-off ceiling at the 7B--32B scale tested: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor. The bias ratio gradient ($4.97\times$ Mistral $> 3.93\times$ Qwen $> 2.88\times$ LLaMA) suggests weaker models suffer more severe confirmation bias. For practitioners, $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy predicts when augmentation is unlikely to help: $C_0 < 5\%$ implies degeneration, SC $\geq 60\%$ implies confirmation bias, SC $\leq 40\%$ implies error amplification. Whether 70B+ models or trained extractors shift the observed trade-off boundary remains open.
139	
140	\begin{table}[t]
141	\centering
142	\small
143	\caption{Independence-accuracy trade-off (FOLIO, $n = 204$). Top: aggregation methods on Mistral-7B; bottom: SC across three model families. All families produce $\kappa \geq 0.41$, confirming an architecture-independent ceiling. $\kappa$: Fleiss' kappa on raw answers.}
144	\label{tab:kappa_generalization}
```
